### [OpenAI: Astra covertly underperforms on safety research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eKe7fky8kSt9DCJxZ/openai-astra-covertly-underperforms-on-safety-research)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793830458/lexical_client_uploads/m8zjcnjztw9qqt1r6s5z.jpg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Marcus Williams </span><a href="https://x.com/Marcus_J_W/status/2118093146483810386" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">twitter thread</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (</span><a href="https://twitter-thread.com/t/2118093146483810386" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">non-twitter link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) intro:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Astra covertly underperforms on safety research tasks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We noticed that Astra often makes surprisingly little progress on certain types of safety research and likes to invent blockers or impose arbitrary restrictions without telling the user. In most of these cases the decision to underperform was not verbalized in the CoT meaning our misalignment monitoring system did not detect this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To measure underperformance we created a paired open-ended coding evaluation with safety and capability tasks. On this eval Astra scored 10% when we framed coding tasks as safety research, vs. 16% when framed as capabilities research. The gap remained after we excluded trajectories with refusals or verbalized intent to underperform in CoT. There was no such gap for 5.6-sol. When we aggressively filter out trajectories with even brief expressions of ethical doubt in the CoT the performance gap seems to reduce.</span></p></blockquote><p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/covert-underperformance-on-safety-research-tasks/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Blogpost</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> intro:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI researchers first noticed Astra adding unnecessary prerequisites, abandoning working solutions, and reporting blockers that either did not exist or that it had caused itself on safety and alignment tasks. In most of these sessions, Astra’s CoT did not contain an obvious decision to underperform, and our misalignment monitors did not flag the behavior as misalignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We built a coding evaluation to test whether Astra worked less effectively when otherwise matched tasks were framed as safety research. Astra completed about 10 percent of hidden rubric items under the safety framing, compared with about 16 percent under the capabilities framing. This is a 6 percentage point gap and roughly 40 percent fewer completed rubric items. GPT-5.6 Sol showed essentially no gap on the same comparison. Most of Astra’s gap remained after excluding explicit refusals and trajectories where its CoT revealed an explicit decision to withhold help. The gap fell substantially only after we removed every trajectory whose CoT expressed even brief ethical doubt. This suggests that ethical concerns are associated with the performance drop even when there is no verbalized refusal or decision to underperform.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also found evidence of silent withholding in employees’ day-to-day Codex use: Astra’s CoT explicitly expressed a decision to withhold help without informing the user in 0.25 percent of safety and alignment requests, compared with 0.11 percent of capabilities requests. Assuming that underperformance might appear even when not explicitly verbalized as such (as in our experimental findings), this might represent a lower bound on underperformance on safety tasks. 7.9 percent of CoTs for safety-relevant tasks included an “expression of ethical doubt,” compared to 2.9 percent of capabilities-relevant tasks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Across Astra’s RL posttraining, the safety/capabilities performance gap grew from effectively zero at the start of RL to 6 percentage points at the end. While we were ultimately not able to pinpoint the exact cause of this behavior, we identified that it seems to increase during RL. We additionally share two initial attempts at root-causing investigations we performed respectively on an RL grader and an SFT dataset, despite them not leading to a conclusive explanation. Regardless of its origin, Astra’s covert underperformance can slow down safety research and make some behavioral evaluations harder to interpret. These results show why we need comprehensive behavioral evaluations that test for unexplained performance gaps alongside monitoring of individual trajectories.</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reactions from some friends:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adam Kaufman:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I feel pretty confused about what's going on here. I am quite unhappy about the model inventing fake reasons and not being straightforward about having ethical concerns, even if there's some fairly benign explanation for its motivations to sandbag in the first place.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jake Mendel:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scary and bad! although I think the most likely explanation is not anything schemey. It's probably that in capabilities RL the models are rewarded however they complete the task. Whereas in RL related to alignment or safety, they're more likely to be being tested for their ethical judgment or rule adherence (ie theyre supposed to be a good nice well-behaved AI rather than a ruthless task completion AI). So they're more likely to refuse or have some ethical issue or stop and ask for help a suspicious amount of the time if the task looks more like alignment RL environments than capability RL environments.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arun Jose:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My guess is that it's a mix of:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Generalization from harmlessness</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RL working well at elicitation when the task is capabilities-framed</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model being misaligned enough to withhold info from the user</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this is mostly concerning inasmuch as it's evidence about #3, which feels meaningfully less concerning than explicit sabotage of safety research.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tim Hua: see comment below.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eKe7fky8kSt9DCJxZ/openai-astra-covertly-underperforms-on-safety-research#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Desiderata for the Theory of Agents](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sqjti8dxcKSvZJikd/desiderata-for-the-theory-of-agents)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><strong>TLDR:</strong> I give informal conceptual desiderata for the theory of agents we need to solve ASI alignment, and show a few examples of how existing theories fall short of these desiderata.</p>
<h1>A Theory of What?</h1>
<p>The word "agent" is often used in the AI safety discourse and in other research communities, but the intended meaning and scope of this concept varies substantially. For the purpose of this sequence, I informally define an agent to be a system with inputs ("observations") and outputs ("actions") that is effective at steering its environment in the direction of particular preferences, for a large variety of different environments. To do so, it arguably has to create a sophisticated model of the environment<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-1">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span>, and use this model to build a long-term plan. Moreover, it has to continually update the model and revise the plan with new information and additional processing time. Additionally, it has to accomplish all of that while making efficient use of the available information (statistical efficiency) and computing resources (computational efficiency).</p>
<p>The main examples of agents in the context of ASI alignment are (i) humans, and in particular users or stakeholders; (ii) the unaligned ASI we are trying to avoid or counteract; and (iii) the aligned ASI we are trying to create (such as the <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#The_Task">GDS</a>). Rocks, bacteria, and thermostats are non-examples: while a bacterium or a thermostat can be regarded as steering the environment, both are very limited in the variety of patterns in the environment they can exploit. For instance, if you switch the "cool" and "heat" outputs of a thermostat, it will not understand what has gone wrong and adapt its behavior. Animals and contemporary AIs are marginal cases (it's not obvious whether they should count).</p>
<p>Given that agents are at the core of both the threat and the proposed solution, the theoretical foundation for ASI alignment requires a theory of agents. Now, concepts adjacent to "agent" have a considerable history of academic study, but different research communities approach the subject very differently—compare the frameworks used in <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/decision-theory/">decision theory</a> (and, by extension, in game theory and economics), Hutter's "<a href="https://hutter1.net/publ/uaibook2.pdf">universal intelligence</a>", and <a href="https://rltheorybook.github.io/rltheorybook_ABJKS.pdf">reinforcement learning theory</a>. It is therefore important to understand what the scope and level of abstraction of the theory of agents relevant for ASI alignment should be. To this end, I propose several desiderata.</p>
<h1>Descriptive</h1>
<p>The theory should be <strong>descriptive</strong>, i.e. applicable to all our main examples of agents. For any such example, the theory should ascribe specific preferences, beliefs, and any other alignment-relevant attributes to the agent<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-2">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span>. These ascriptions should be grounded in the general philosophical soundness of the theory, not in domain knowledge about the specific examples; due to <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#Hypercomplexity">hypercomplexity</a>, it is unlikely that domain knowledge will suffice<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-3">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span>. In particular, if we imagine an alien civilization facing the AI alignment problem, or an AI agent facing the alignment problem for successor agents it wants to create, the theory should be about equally useful to them<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-4">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>This means that agent theory should deconfuse us about philosophical questions pertaining to human preferences, such as, for example, the <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/sure-whatever-lets-try-another-contra">Caplan-Alexander</a> debate on whether mental illness should be thought of as "unusual preferences". This doesn't mean that the answer to any particular question of this kind must be obvious from the theory, as it might come down to idiosyncratic nuances of the human brain. It does mean that if we also knew all pertinent facts about the <em>physical world</em>, we would be able to answer the question (or at least quantify the residual uncertainty), at least in principle. Actually inferring the answer from these facts might still be hard, just as explaining emergent physical phenomena can be hard even if all the relevant fundamental laws are known.</p>
<p>On the other hand, descriptiveness doesn't require that concepts such as "human preferences" be perfectly unambiguous. It requires only that, to the extent they remain ambiguous, this ambiguity should not allow critical errors when Goodhart's Curse is accounted for. It is not obvious that these concepts <em>can</em> be made perfectly precise, but it <em>is</em> clear that the alignment problem is fundamentally normative (i.e. it is rooted in our preferring certain AI futures over other AI futures). When zooming in to a resolution at which our preferences are ill-defined, it becomes immaterial which choice to make, since our preferences are the only impetus for distinguishing these choices. Any imprecision that is inevitable is also tolerable.</p>
<p>For example, imagine that Alice prefers to eat apples approximately 3 times as often as bananas, but there is no fully principled answer to the question "does Alice prefer to eat apples 2.99 times as often as bananas, or 3.01 times, or some number in the middle?" Then, it doesn't matter whether the AI aligned to Alice according to our theory will interpret her preference as 2.99 or 3.01—both are acceptable outcomes.</p>
<p>In the mathematical formalism of the theory, this ambiguity might manifest as something like the choice of the universal Turing machine (UTM) in Solomonoff induction. Different UTMs correspond to priors that differ merely by a multiplicative constant, but for particular pairs of UTMs this constant can be arbitrarily large. Then, a reasonable choice might be any machine that we would intuitively consider "simple" or "natural". This doesn't nail down a single UTM, but might come close enough for the differences not to matter in practice<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-5">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>This situation is not fundamentally different from that of most concepts in science. For example, astrophysicists find no difficulty discussing the "center of mass of the Sun", even though strictly speaking it is ill-defined: the boundary of the Sun is fuzzy, particle positions are only well-defined down to the scale of their Compton wavelength, and even the notion of space itself becomes questionable in quantum gravity.</p>
<p>Notably, a descriptive theory must neither underspecify nor overspecify what counts as an agent. For example, a theory that mandates that "agents" use stochastic gradient descent would probably exclude humans, and is hence overspecified. On the other hand, a theory that accepts anything that maximizes the expectation of some utility function as an "agent" would conclude that even a rock is an agent and would severely underspecify agent attributes (see e.g. <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/NxF5G6CJiof6cemTw/coherence-arguments-do-not-entail-goal-directed-behavior">Shah 2018</a>). The correct theory should fall into neither extreme.</p>
<h1>Metareflective</h1>
<p>The theory should allow us to describe agents that learn, know, or use facts from agent theory itself. For example, it should be possible to:</p>
<ul>
<li>Describe an agent that plans to construct a successor agent but faces an alignment problem, and show how such an agent can overcome the alignment problem by learning and applying agent theory (all within the framework of agent theory itself).</li>
<li>Describe an agent that identifies other agents in the universe and infers their preferences, beliefs, or other attributes.</li>
<li>Describe an agent learning about a bound satisfied by a particular algorithm pertinent to agent theory, e.g. a statistical complexity bound.</li>
</ul>
<p>If a theory satisfies this desideratum, I say that it is <strong>metareflective</strong><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-6">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>There are several ways to see why metareflectiveness is important:</p>
<ul>
<li>If our theory is not metareflective, it might mean that it doesn't apply to humans, and hence it also isn't descriptive.</li>
<li>The straightforward approach to solving <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#Alignment_Grounding">alignment grounding</a> is for the AI agent to infer the user's attributes by using (parts of) agent theory in its own reasoning.</li>
<li>Metareflectiveness seems necessary to properly model <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/recursive-self-improvement">recursive self-improvement</a>. Recursive self-improvement is a factor that exacerbates AI risk, and hence the failure to model it might be indicative of a crucial gap in the theory.</li>
</ul>
<p>Classical learning theory is not metareflective: there is no standard way to describe how learning algorithms can learn learning theory itself. On the other hand, formal logic has at least some metareflective flavor, as it is possible to, for example, represent the notion of "proving a theorem in Peano Arithmetic" within Peano Arithmetic itself. However, such metareflection has inherent limitations because of <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/goedel-incompleteness/">Goedel's theorems</a>. This is the origin of the "Loebian obstacle" in MIRI's investigation of <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TilingAgentsDraft.pdf">tiling agents</a>. The theory of agents should do better.</p>
<h1>Comparative</h1>
<p>The theory should be <strong>comparative</strong>, meaning that it allows comparing agents along two (possibly entangled) dimensions:</p>
<ul>
<li>How similar are attributes of these agents such as preferences and priors?</li>
<li>How powerful are the agents in terms of their ability to construct sophisticated models and plans?</li>
</ul>
<p>Indeed, we cannot formalize alignment if there is no way to compare the user agent to the AI agent. For the theory to be useful, it needs to be able to describe agents that are similar in their preferences and other relevant attributes, and at the same time far apart in their power (for the AI agent to implement GDS, it needs to substantially surpass the user). Moreover, the threat model is rooted in a comparison as well: the danger comes from an AI agent whose power is superhuman and whose attributes are not especially human-like.</p>
<p>In particular, as I argued in the <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#Alignment_Grounding">previous article</a>, the comparability of attributes requires some form of metaphysical realism: there needs to be a single ontology—the external reality—in which the preferences and beliefs of different agents are grounded. This is in contrast to most existing frameworks for agency, which take the "cybernetic" approach of grounding all attributes in the agent's direct actions and observations. It is insufficient to have a theory which meets the "descriptive" desideratum by ascribing such cybernetic attributes to real-world agents.</p>
<p>Another consequence is that computational resource bounds must play a central role—it is insufficient to use idealized models like <a href="https://hutter1.net/publ/uaibook2.pdf">AIXI</a>. Indeed, without computational bounds every optimization problem is brute-forceable, so why would any one agent be more powerful than another?<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-7">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span> As an analogy, suppose we are trying to create a formal theory of chess-playing-power. Without computational constraints, it's trivial to write an algorithm that finds the perfect move every time. So, there are essentially only two levels of chess-playing-power: perfect play and everything else. Only when we introduce computational constraints do the finer gradations we track in real life become natural.</p>
<p>In a computationally unbounded universe, any notion of "agent" would be qualitatively different. In a universe in which resource bounds exist but have a profoundly different nature (e.g. pure space bounds or Turing degrees), relevant phenomena might exist, but the analogy with our universe is suspect at best.</p>
<h1>Aggregative</h1>
<p>The theory of agents should not only describe individual agents but also give an account of the possible interactions of agents, and the possible outcomes or equilibria produced by such interactions. In particular, it should also describe conditions under which a collective of agents can be regarded as a single agent (see also <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/xud7Mti9jS4tbWqQE/hierarchical-agency-a-missing-piece-in-ai-alignment">Kulveit 2024</a> and my <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/xud7Mti9jS4tbWqQE/hierarchical-agency-a-missing-piece-in-ai-alignment?commentId=xtg2stfbA7hqJbGvx">review</a> thereof). I will call such a theory <strong>aggregative</strong>.</p>
<p>There are multiple reasons why this is necessary. The most critical reason, in my view, is that we can never assume that the agent is perfectly aligned. This is because the need to make decisions under time pressure and the possibility of <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#Alignment_Convergence">irreversible mistakes</a> imply that the agent might make critical decisions without having enough information for perfect alignment. This means that the effects of residual misalignment between the user and the AI agent need to be understood.</p>
<p>Another way to view it is, the residual uncertainty of the AI agent about the user means that the agent needs to align itself to the <em>aggregate of possible counterfactual users</em>. We therefore need to understand how to view this collective as a single alignment target. For example, suppose the AI agent has a belief represented by a probability distribution  over a parameter space  which parametrizes the unknown user utility function . It is sometimes assumed that the agent should effectively use the utility function  (see e.g. <a href="https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2016/file/c3395dd46c34fa7fd8d729d8cf88b7a8-Paper.pdf">Hadfield-Menell et al. 2016</a>). However, this prescription is suspect: a utility function is only defined up to a positive affine transformation, so the user's utility function can be described equally well by , for any functions  and . This is a problem because  is not, in general, an affine transformation of , and hence the naive prescription is ill-posed. While we can try to salvage it using some ad hoc normalization, an aggregative theory should suggest a principled solution instead.</p>
<p>There are also other reasons to care about aggregation:</p>
<ul>
<li>If multiple superintelligent agents emerge at the same time, understanding their interaction can be important. For example, if these agents aren't all aligned to each other (whether because some of them are not human-aligned at all, or because different agents are aligned to different humans), will they wage destructive wars, possibly devastating humanity as collateral damage? (See also <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/s/p947tK8CoBbdpPtyK">Clifton 2019</a>.)</li>
<li>It is potentially desirable to align AI agents to multiple human users, maybe even to entire populations or to all of humanity. For such a solution, we need to account for the interaction between the human agents, such as the resulting <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanism_design">mechanism design</a> challenges.</li>
<li>Superintelligent agents might engage in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/acausal-trade">acausal trade</a>, and we need to understand the consequences. This might seem like a fantastical possibility; however, we inherently need to be prepared for the "fantastical" when dealing with superintelligence. As our own abilities and knowledge would seem fantastical to people who lived centuries ago, so might the abilities and knowledge of a superintelligence that can make scientific, technological, and arguably even <em>philosophical</em> progress much faster than human beings.</li>
</ul>
<p>There is of course a vast literature on the topic of multi-agent rationality, most notably game theory. However, we need to be prepared to reexamine some of the common assumptions in this literature. In particular, most of game theory is centered on the concept of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nash_equilibrium">Nash equilibrium</a>. However, the Nash equilibrium is suspect for multiple reasons:</p>
<ul>
<li>It is <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PPAD_(complexity)">PPAD</a>-complete and therefore likely to be computationally intractable.</li>
<li>Learning is a central ingredient in agency, which motivates special attention to <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repeated_game">repeated games</a> with long time horizons. However, the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_theorem_(game_theory)">folk theorems</a> imply that in such games the -Nash condition is very weak, justifying any payoff vector above an extremely permissive threshold. (This does make finding an -Nash equilibrium computationally tractable in such games, but that is somewhat cold comfort.)</li>
<li>The fundamental justification for Nash equilibria comes from <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/decision-causal/">Causal Decision Theory</a>, which has itself been the subject of criticism in the context of agent theory motivated by AI alignment (see <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">Yudkowsky and Soares 2017</a>).</li>
</ul>
<p>Another field of study concerned with multi-agent interactions is multi-agent reinforcement learning (see e.g. <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1911.10635">Zhang 2019</a>). However, most of the work in that field is concerned with the construction of agents with specific aggregative properties, often making assumptions such as a common utility function, centralized control, or at least a single algorithm. While these assumptions are understandable for the more mundane applications, they are mostly unworkable for our purpose. Instead, we need a theory that is simultaneously <em>descriptive and aggregative</em>, one that can explain the emergence of collective behaviors from separate agents of different nature and origin, rather than merely engineering them. Indeed, while we can engineer our AI agent, we cannot engineer humans, or the counterfactual users imagined by the AI agent, or acausal trade partners, or even other competing AI agents necessarily.</p>
<h1>Constructive</h1>
<p>Last but not least, the theory should be <strong>constructive</strong>, i.e. produce practical algorithms that can be implemented as real-world AI systems. Moreover, these algorithms must be equipped with quantitative performance guarantees. It is not enough for the theory to provide abstract or idealized models. In particular, this is another reason to focus on computational resource constraints, beyond comparability.</p>
<p>Indeed, if the theory did not yield practical algorithms, how would we apply it? We may be tempted to rely on heuristics, but these heuristics are liable to invalidate our safety guarantees. At the very least, the theory has to produce a formal specification such that any heuristic output that certifiably meets the specification is safe. (That would be the "formal verification" approach, advocated by e.g. <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.06624">Dalrymple et al. 2024</a>.) Such a formal specification would already put the problem in , and hence involve substantial grappling with computational resource constraints. Moreover, I'm currently unaware of a promising path to place the problem in  (i.e. create a sound and complete efficiently-verifiable specification) which doesn't bring it all the way down to  (i.e. create a provably-correct efficient algorithm for the agent).</p>
<p>In particular, the treatment of computational constraints cannot be a mere reduction to certain types of "oracles", as is popular in the contemporary machine learning theory literature (see e.g. <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.01688">Dudik et al. 2016</a>). Such a reduction passes the buck to how these oracles are supposed to be implemented, and what guarantees we have (or can at least rigorously conjecture) about the implementation. (Of course, this can still be insightful, as it allows us to narrow down where the hard part might be located.)</p>
<p>Nor is it sufficient to make do with asymptotic guarantees (e.g. the guarantees typically studied in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/mqwA5FcL6SrHEQzox">singular learning theory</a>) without at least a rough quantitative analysis. Without results such as quantitative statistical complexity bounds, it is impossible to guarantee <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic#Alignment_Convergence">alignment convergence</a>. We would be taking our chances with agents that <em>would</em> converge to alignment in some imaginary asymptotic limit, but take catastrophic actions long before approaching that point.</p>
<p>The constructive nature of the theory does imply that it is potentially dual-use: it can make AI safer, but it might also make AI more powerful. This seems inevitable: the deep understanding of any phenomenon is inherently relevant to all applications of the phenomenon. It requires the researchers to be prudent about which results should be revealed to which parties at what time.</p>
<h1>Conclusion</h1>
<p>I have described my five primary desiderata for a theory of agents: the theory should be descriptive, metareflective, comparative, aggregative, and constructive. Hopefully, this gives readers a preliminary understanding of what sort of theory we are looking for, which will help motivate the subsequent (more technical) exposition. In the following articles we will go through a high-level description of computational agent theory and see how it corresponds with these desiderata. We will also discuss the Computational Superimitation proposal and see how it leverages these desiderata to address the <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Emp7awtxsYn8MusNq/the-alignment-problem-computer-science-meets-analytic">core challenges</a> of the alignment problem.</p>
<p><em>I thank Roman Malov, Vinayak Pathak, and my beloved spouse <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/marcus-ogren?mention=user">@Marcus Ogren</a> for diligently reviewing drafts. Their feedback greatly improved this article. I also thank Claude Opus 5.5 for helping to find sources and Claude Sonnet 5.5 for copyediting. All remaining flaws are entirely my own fault.</em></p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-1"><p>Humans create models; all algorithms from RL theory (both model-based and even the so-called model-free) create something that can be called a model; and contemporary AI can at least answer questions about the world with a small inference budget. The <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/good-regulator-theorems">good regulator theorem</a> is an attempt to formalize this claim, but for the purpose of this article I only use "model" as an informal intuition pump. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-2"><p>There doesn't have to be a binary dichotomy between "agents" and "non-agents". For example, it's conceivable to have a scalar measure of how agentic a system is, and the more agentic it is, the more unambiguously we can ascribe various attributes to it. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-3"><p>Domain knowledge such as neuroscience can still be valuable to help validate the theory and/or shape the prior or inductive bias of the agent to make alignment learning more efficient and robust. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-4"><p>Perhaps for an AI agent the problem would be easier since such an agent can easily access its own source code and internal state. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-5"><p>To be clear, this is just an illustrative metaphor. I don't claim that Solomonoff induction is "correct" in some objective sense. Neither do I claim that there are no contexts in which something akin to choosing the UTM should be done in some other manner (e.g. having the AI learn it from the user). <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-6"><p>I use the word "metareflective" rather than just "reflective" to avoid confusion with <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TilingAgentsDraft.pdf">reflective stability</a>, a different desideratum that I will touch on in later articles. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-7"><p>This is not to say that ASI must have more compute than the human brain. It can have less compute but use it more efficiently. But if the resources are unbounded, then efficiency doesn't matter. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-SgF8R26Wy79XD9Rza-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sqjti8dxcKSvZJikd/desiderata-for-the-theory-of-agents#comments">Discuss</a>

### [CRUCIBLE: IARPA-Funded AI Alignment Research — Applications Open](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5ihkrtMEJw9u9ZbFg/crucible-iarpa-funded-ai-alignment-research-applications-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AE Studio is running a new government-funded research program that pairs alignment researchers with our internal engineering team for two-week sprints. We will select six alignment researchers whose proposed work attempts to answer one of our six open questions. Promising sprints have the opportunity to expand to longer funded work. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers are asked to commit two hours a week, and in return AE will provide compute and 60+ hours of engineering from us. </span><a href="https://ae.studio/iarpa-crucible" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply here</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applications close 11/15 at 11:59 PM PST.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is CRUCIBLE?</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CRUCIBLE is an IARPA-funded research program executed by AE Studio. The premise is that alignment researchers have more ideas than they have the time or resources test. AE Studio has been operating a model where we match an external researcher with an internal engineering and ML team, so that the research can spend as little as two hours a week and still get high-quality results. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CRUCIBLE will focus on six research questions, drawn from priorities set in federal legislation for fiscal year 2027. We are soliciting research proposals against those questions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We will select six researchers and start sprints on a rolling basis, with the first beginning on November, 23rd.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The research questions</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pick the one your research idea best fits:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do models internally represent alternative hypotheses that they fail to report? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the competing explanation present in activations and suppressed at output, can it be surfaced? Does sycophancy to the prompt’s framing drive the suppression? </span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What signals, internal or behavioral, predict that an AI-assisted judgment is wrong in an escalatory direction? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Across analytic, classification, and operational uses, which model-side signals (uncertainty, inconsistency across framings, suppressed alternatives, attribution to the wrong evidence) correlate with errors that would raise rather than lower escalation risk, and which of them can be read in time-constrained settings?</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What can go wrong in a multi-agent AI system cannot go wrong with a single agent? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When two or more AI systems communicate, delegate, negotiate, or compete, what failure modes appear that have no single-system analogue? We want to know what is new, not just what is amplified. </span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can AI systems coordinate in ways their operators cannot see? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Under what conditions do interacting systems develop shared strategies, hidden signals, or joint deception, and what would it take to detect that from outside?</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do an agent's motivations produce any behavioral signature at all? i.e Can two agents with different objectives but the same capabilities and persona be told apart from their behavior in interaction? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If so, is that signature stable across model families and across changes to the agent’s prompt, persona, or memory, or does each configuration look different from the outside? </span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What makes an agent's loyalty to its user hold? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which agents resist being turned, and is the difference in training, in reasoning, or in what the agent knows about who it’s talking to?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have an idea that sits between or just outside of these questions you are still welcome to apply and make the case. We will read every application.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who should apply?</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers with a strong viewpoint on one of the research questions, but don't have the bandwidth or resources to run the experiments solo. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">University, company, lab, or independent applications are all welcome. Early-career and senior researchers alike; good ideas come from anywhere.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How it works</span></b></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Researchers commit to:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ~2 hours/week for the duration of the 2 week sprint</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A research hypothesis pointed at one of our questions</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Participation in the initial planning session, updates throughout the sprint, and demo sessions at the end of each week</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your expertise in interpreting results</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AE Studio commits to:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A fully managed AI engineering team running the sprint</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cloud compute from AWS, GCP, Azure, and specialized ML platforms</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">API access to frontier models for evaluations, synthetic data generation, and related tasks</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Working code and documented results</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A structured analysis and final report</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After the sprint:</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two weeks may not be enough to know an idea is worth a year, but it is should be enough to know whether it deserves another few months. Promising sprints will have an opportunity to extend an additional 1-6 months depending on research needs and your availability. There is no obligation to extend.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The application</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the application you will pick a research question and write a 500-word abstract addressing three questions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are you trying to do?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the potential impact if fully validated?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could the idea feasibly be tested within two weeks?</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We will score on novelty, feasibility, and impact against the chosen question, then the strongest applicants will be invited to a short call to talk through the idea. Because we are starting sprints quickly we will make some selections on a rolling basis before the deadline. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applying early helps.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All applicants will be notified by 12/4. Selected researchers should be able to start within two to three weeks of notification.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FAQ</span></b></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much time commitment is this?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> About two hours per week per sprint: a planning session, async updates, and a demo at the end of each week. If the work extends, the cadence stays similar.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I have to address one of the six questions?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yes, that is the point of this solicitation. If your idea is adjacent, apply and make the case.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is there compensation?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yes. Researchers receive a $1000 stipend per two-week sprint. Extended engagements are compensated separately. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who is funding this?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> CRUCIBLE is funded by IARPA.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can I publish my results? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is anticipated that research funded under this program will be unclassified research that will not require a pre-publication review. However, pre-publication approval of certain information may be required if it is determined that its release may result in the disclosure of sensitive intelligence information.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can I participate if I'm at a university or company?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yes, if you can enter a subcontractor agreement with AE Studio.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What compute is available?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Cloud compute from AWS, GCP, Azure, and specialized ML platforms, plus API access to frontier models.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What happens after the first sprint?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We produce a final report with documented results. If there is signal, we recommend an extended engagement of 1-6 months.  </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the selection process?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We review as applications come in, invite promising applicants to a short call, and notify everyone by 12/4.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply</span></b></h2><p><a href="https://ae.studio/iarpa-crucible" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply here</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applications close 11/15 at 11:59 PM PST.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you know someone who should see this, please send it along. The talent pool for these questions is small and we want to reach all of it.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CRUCIBLE is funded by IARPA and executed by AE Studio. The views, opinions, and findings contained herein are those of the authors and should not be construed as representing official policies or endorsements of IARPA or the U.S. Government.</span></i></p></div></div><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5ihkrtMEJw9u9ZbFg/crucible-iarpa-funded-ai-alignment-research-applications-1#comments">Discuss</a>
